### [The worthlessness of vitamin D is mildly exaggerated](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sF5gAxnmifQe2TBNt/the-worthlessness-of-vitamin-d-is-mildly-exaggerated)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>For a while there, many people thought vitamin D was magical—that it could improve bones, the heart, infections, cancer, heart disease, longevity, even mental health. But among people I respect, opinion is now overwhelmingly that taking vitamin D does nothing unless you're severely deficient. The central argument is that while vitamin D levels are correlated with ~all positive health outcomes, when you actually test vitamin D supplements against placebo in randomized trials, nothing ever happens.</span></p><p><span>That's what I used to think, too. But I've come to think the skeptics have over-corrected. Yes, randomized trials have shown the magical correlations are not causal. But if you start with non-insane expectations, the trials look like weak but positive evidence. And if you consider what we know about biology and evolution, I think the balance of evidence tips pretty clearly in the direction that people with low-ish levels would be wise to supplement.</span></p><p><span>Am I certain that vitamin D is beneficial for people with low-ish levels? Absolutely not! But I claim that's the best bet given the limits of our knowledge.</span></p><h2><span>The classical view: Boring bone vitamin</span></h2><p><span>Most vitamins are "ingredients" that the body uses to do stuff. Vitamin D is more like a "signal" that the body uses to communicate with itself about </span><i><span>what</span></i><span> to do.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span>The classical "endocrine" story of vitamin D is that your body uses it to tell your guts to take in more calcium from food. If you don't get enough vitamin D, then you have calcium problems.</span></p><p><span>That's all you really need to know about the classical view. But if you enjoy gawking at biology's complexity, I recommend this diagram and the following three paragraphs:</span></p><img alt="pathways.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782242860/lexical_client_uploads/nhfmqitewc7aqvmla8if.png" /><p><span>Ready for science? OK: Almost all the cells in your body make </span><b><span>provitamin D</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span>Usually, this is all converted to cholesterol, but your skin cells leave some sitting around. When UVB light hits those skin cells, provitamin D is transformed (physically by the light itself) into </span><b><span>previtamin D</span></b><span> and then (by heat) into </span><b><span>vitamin D</span></b><span>. This diffuses from the skin cells into blood vessels. There it binds to a protein</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span>and starts circulating in the blood, where it is joined by vitamin D from food.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-4">[4]</a></sup></span><span>Eventually, the liver converts it into more-stable </span><b><span>storage vitamin D</span></b><span>. It also soaks in and out of fat and muscle tissue, which acts as a slow-release reservoir.</span></p><p><span>Now, a fun fact: If calcium levels in your blood get too low, then your heart will stop working and you will die. To avoid this, you have </span><b><span>parathyroid glands</span></b><span> in your neck that sense when calcium is getting low, and release </span><b><span>parathyroid hormone</span></b><span> into the blood. This tells your bones to release some of their stored calcium. It also tells your </span><b><span>kidneys</span></b><span> to convert some of the storage vitamin D from your blood into </span><b><span>active vitamin D</span></b><span>. And when </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> gets to your guts, they try to absorb more calcium from food.</span></p><p><span>So what happens if you don't get enough vitamin D? Well, your body is not going to let calcium levels drop too low, because your body is designed to avoid death. Parathyroid hormone will still get secreted, and it will still tell your bones to scavenge calcium. But without vitamin D, your guts never get the signal to gather extra calcium from food. So the body scavenges a </span><i><span>lot</span></i><span> of calcium from your bones, and you end up with weak bones, which is bad.</span></p><p><span>Now here's the thing: In this story, only active vitamin D actually does anything. The kidneys make this on demand in response to calcium levels, </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> in response to storage vitamin D levels. </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5393439"><span>General opinion</span></a><span> is that as long as the blood has above ~25 nmol/L of storage vitamin D, then the kidneys have no trouble making active vitamin D.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-5">[5]</a></sup></span><span>Furthermore, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/s8756-3282(02)00692-0"><span>survey data</span></a><span> suggests that only ~2% of the population has levels below that threshold. This suggests that for ~98% of people, supplementing vitamin D should do approximately nothing.</span></p><h2><span>The correlation view: Magical mystery cure</span></h2><p><span>Rickets is a terrible disease that involves soft bones, stunted growth, and skeletal deformities. It's probably been with us since ancient times, but it became common in the West after the industrial revolution. In 1890, a Scottish missionary named Theobald Palm </span><a href="https://babel.hathitrust.org/cgi/pt?id=pst.32239000047437&amp;seq=288"><span>observed</span></a><span> that rickets was common in smog-ridden UK cities but almost unheard of in sunny countries with poor sanitation, suggesting sunlight itself was the issue. This contributed to the discovery that rickets could be cured with UV light or cod-liver oil, and eventually the discovery of vitamin D.</span></p><p><span>In 1941, Apperly </span><a href="https://aacrjournals.org/cancerres/article/1/3/191/472224/The-Relation-of-Solar-Radiation-to-Cancer"><span>noticed</span></a><span> that the amount of sunlight in different US states was positively correlated with skin cancer but </span><i><span>inversely</span></i><span> correlated with overall cancer mortality.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span>He gave this charming graph:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 66.31419939577039%;"><img alt="apperly-rot.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782242877/lexical_client_uploads/bviwz42qzja7nvobfd6y.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>Apperly never mentions vitamin D, presumably because he thought it was a boring bone vitamin.</span></p><p><span>Things took off in 1980, when Cedric and Frank Garland published, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1093/ije/9.3.227"><span>"Do Sunlight and Vitamin D Reduce the Likelihood of Colon Cancer?"</span></a><span> Seemingly unaware of Apperly, they gave a similar, but uglier, graph:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 63.0488415859257%;"><img alt="garland.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782242888/lexical_client_uploads/ndm2orqjc1nvjdfocvy5.png" /></figure><p><span>They point out that regional diets (like meat and fiber) didn't seem to explain this pattern. Instead, they propose a mechanistic story:</span></p><p><span>    Sunlight</span><br /><span>        ↓</span><br /><span>    Vitamin D</span><br /><span>        ↓</span><br /><span>    Adequate calcium in blood</span><br /><span>        ↓</span><br /><span>    Reduced inflammation of epithelial cells in the colon</span><br /><span>        ↓</span><br /><span>    Less colon cancer</span></p><p><span>(It's always inflammation.) This paper was rejected many times before finally being published. I wish I could find an un-gated copy to link to, because it would have made a magnificent blog post.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-7">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Following that paper, there was an explosion of work that found negative correlations between sunlight (or latitude) and other types of </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ejca.2012.11.001"><span>cancers</span></a><span> as well as </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1161/01.HYP.30.2.150"><span>blood pressure</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1007/s00125-008-1061-5"><span>diabetes</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1136/jnnp.2011.240432"><span>multiple sclerosis</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Then people started measuring vitamin D in blood. In 1989, the Garlands and collaborators </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(89)91789-3"><span>found</span></a><span> blood samples takin in 1974 from 25,000 people. They found that 34 of those people had since gotten colon cancer. They matched these with 67 demographically similar people and measured vitamin D levels in the stored blood samples for all 101 people. Among that group, people with vitamin D levels below 50 nmol/L got colon cancer more than three times as often as people with higher levels.</span></p><p><span>Again, many similar studies followed. These linked higher vitamin D levels to better outcomes in cardiovascular disease, diabetes, obesity, infectious disease, Parkinson's, and mood disorders. While results were mixed for non-colorectal cancer incidence, higher vitamin D levels predicted better survival of many cancers. Amazingly, </span><i><span>all-cause mortality</span></i><span> was roughly 30% lower for those at the 75th percentile of vitamin D levels compared to the 25th.</span></p><p><span>Vitamin D was looking like a miracle. But how could it do all that stuff if it was just a boring bone vitamin?</span></p><h2><span>Meanwhile in biology</span></h2><p><span>While all these correlations were being discovered, we learned that the body doesn't just use vitamin D for bone stuff.</span></p><p><span>In 1969, we discovered the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitamin_D_receptor"><span>vitamin D receptor</span></a><span> that active vitamin D binds to in the gut and bones. And in the 1980s came a shock: Almost all cells in the body have vitamin D receptors. These seem to do different things in different tissues. In the pancreas, they support insulin secretion. In immune cells, they boost antimicrobial peptides and reduce inflammation. In neurons, they influence proliferation and differentiation.</span></p><p><span>So… What? When calcium drops and the kidneys put out active vitamin D, does every part of the body start doing different unrelated stuff?</span></p><p><span>In the late 1990s, we cloned the gene for the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25-Hydroxyvitamin_D_1-alpha-hydroxylase"><span>enzyme</span></a><span> that the kidneys use to convert storage vitamin D to active vitamin D. Soon came another shock: This enzyme also exists in tons of other cells, including immune cells, the heart, the skin, the prostate, the breast, and colon. (Another win for the Garlands.)</span></p><p><span>So it's not just the kidneys making active vitamin D to trigger the gut. Cells everywhere are making their </span><i><span>own</span></i><span> active vitamin D and using it to trigger vitamin D receptors in neighboring cells, or even inside the same cell.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-8">[8]</a></sup></span><span>This often has little to do with calcium or bones.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-9">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The kidneys use vitamin D as a boring bone hormone.</span></li><li value="2"><span>As long as the blood contains at least ~25 nmol/L of storage vitamin D, the kidneys don't care. They create the same amount of active vitamin D, in response to calcium levels.</span></li><li value="3"><span>But now cells everywhere are using storage vitamin D.</span></li><li value="4"><span>To do god-knows-what.</span></li><li value="5"><span>With god-knows-what sensitivity to circulating vitamin D levels.</span></li></ol><p><span>And remember how only active vitamin D does anything? That's wrong. In the mid-1970s, we learned that </span><i><span>storage</span></i><span> vitamin D also binds to the vitamin D receptor. The affinity is 100-1000× lower, but have ~1000× more in your blood. So maybe circulating levels of storage vitamin D themselves matter, independently of how much active vitamin D gets made?</span></p><p><span>If that's not confusing enough, people also noticed that while active vitamin D levels in the blood aren't correlated with storage vitamin D (above ~25 nmol/L), levels of </span><i><span>parathyroid hormone</span></i><span> (the thing your parathyroid glands use to tell your kidneys to make active vitamin D) seem to decline as levels of storage vitamin D rise from ~25 to 50 or 75 nmol/L. Huh?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-10">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>On the one hand, all this makes the idea that vitamin D could be a miracle more plausible. On the other hand, this is getting complicated. And do we really believe that raising your vitamin D levels from the 25th to the 75th percentile could reduce your risk of death from </span><i><span>any cause</span></i><span> by </span><i><span>thirty percent</span></i><span>? Maybe we should try giving people vitamin D and see what happens.</span></p><h2><span>Then came the RCTs</span></h2><p><span>There have been </span><i><span>many</span></i><span> randomized trials. The "right" thing to do in such cases is to look at meta analyses that carefully combine all the data. We'll get to those. But they conceal a lot of important nuance about what actually happens on the ground during these trials. So let's start by going over the three main "megatrials".</span></p><p><span>The Women's Health Initiative (WHI) trial came out in 2006 and is still the largest vitamin D trial ever done. This took 36,000 postmenopausal American women and assigned half to take 400 IU daily with calcium and the other half to placebo.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-11">[11]</a></sup></span><span>After seven years, here's what happened:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-12">[12]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Outcome (WHI trial)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Hazard ratio</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Fractures</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.97 (0.91 to 1.03)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.97 (0.91 to 1.04)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.90 (0.77 to 1.05)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>CVD mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.94 (0.78 to 1.12)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.92 (0.83 to 1.01)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Kidney stones</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.17 (1.02 to 1.34)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>(The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazard_ratio"><span>hazard ratio</span></a><span> is the ratio of the rate that something happens in the treatment vs. placebo groups. So, a number less than one suggests a benefit to taking vitamin D, while a number larger than one suggests a harm. The numbers in parentheses show a 95% confidence interval.)</span></p><p><span>The only statistically significant result was a bad one: Extra kidney stones, likely from the extra calcium.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-13">[13]</a></sup></span><span>The other outcomes look vaguely good, but none were statistically significant despite the massive sample size.</span></p><p><span>This was disappointing. However, the WHI trial had limitations: Many subjects in both the vitamin D and placebo groups were already taking vitamin D, and continued taking it through the trial. The dose of 400 IU was fairly low, many subjects stopped taking their pills, and vitamin D levels didn't actually change that much. They also measured vitamin D levels in only 6% of subjects, meaning we can't compare the fates of subjects who started out with low versus high levels.</span></p><p><span>The next big hope was VITAL, which came out in 2018. They recruited 26,000 older people across the United States, half of them men and 20% Black (and thus </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ajcnut.2025.09.046"><span>far more likely</span></a><span> to be vitamin-D deficient). They measured vitamin D levels in most people, and they gave the treatment group 2,000 IU per day.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-14">[14]</a></sup></span><span>Here were the results after 5.3 years:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Outcome (VITAL trial)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Hazard ratio</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Diabetes</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.91 (0.76 to 1.09)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Autoimmune disease</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2021-066452"><span>0.78</span></a><span> (0.61 to 0.99)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.96 (0.88 to 1.06)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.83 (0.67 to 1.02)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Major CVD event</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.97 (0.85 to 1.12)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>CVD mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.11 (0.88 to 1.40)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.99 (0.87 to 1.12)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Some of the results look good-ish, but cardiovascular mortality was higher in the treatment group, leading to almost no effect on all-cause mortality.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-15">[15]</a></sup></span><span>More disappointment.</span></p><p><span>The last megatrial was D-Health, which came out in 2022 based on 21,000 older Australians. Instead of daily supplements, it used a monthly "bolus" dose of 60,000 IU or placebo. Unlike in VITAL, there was no exclusion for people with a history of cardiovascular disease or cancer, and less restriction on how much vitamin D participants could take on their own during the trial.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-16"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-16">[16]</a></sup></span><span>Here were the results after 6 years:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Outcome (D-Health trial)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Hazard ratio</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.15 (0.96 to 1.39)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Major CVD event</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.91 (0.81 to 1.01)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>CVD mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.96 (0.72 to 1.28)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.04 (0.93 to 1.18)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Now, the treatment group did better in terms of cardiovascular disease, but worse in cancer and worse in all-cause mortality. Even more disappointment.</span></p><p><span>Just from these three large trials, the main lesson should already be clear: Vitamin D is not a miracle. The correlations were wrong.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-17"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-17">[17]</a></sup></span><span>There is essentially zero remaining hope that taking vitamin D could reduce all-cause mortality by a third.</span></p><p><span>In this sense, the vitamin D skeptics are definitely right. But what about the other trials? And is there a more subtle lesson?</span></p><h2><span>I made some tables</span></h2><p><span>I wanted a big table that summarized all the major vitamin D RCTs and what they found for different health outcomes. Annoyingly, no such overview appears to exist. So I made my own:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-18">[18]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell mortality</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>CVD</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>CVD mortality</span></p></th><th class="table-cell hazard ratios are less than one, suggesting a benefit to supplementation. But lots of them are also higher than one, suggesting a harm. The numbers that are far from one almost always come from smaller trials, which manifest as larger confidence intervals. If you're interested in the details of how these trials were run, I refer you to more gigantic tables in a footnote.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-19">[19]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>If big tables aren't your thing, here are some formal 1.03)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span>Squinting at the data</span></h2><p><span>There are various ways you could try to squint at these RCT. In almost all of them, most people already had pretty high levels before they started. So why don't we separate out people who started low? Usually we can't, because most trials didn't measure baseline vitamin D.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-20"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-20">[20]</a></sup></span><span>And among the trials that did, there are few people with low levels, so the results are noisy and confusing.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-21"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-21">[21]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Or, you might theorize that benefits would take time to show up, meaning the first couple years just add noise. In some cases—notably VITAL—excluding the first two years seems to help, but in other cases things get worse.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-22"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-22">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Finally, some people speculate that taking gigantic monthly or quarterly "bolus" doses of vitamin D might be dangerous. For example, here's an enjoyable paragraph from Kunzia et al. in their meta-analysis of vitamin D and cancer mortality:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Our results showing efficacy of daily, but not bolus, vitamin D3 supplementation in reducing cancer mortality are consistent with previous meta-analyses on cancer mortality or all-cause mortality (Guo et al., 2022; Keum et al., 2022; Keum et al., 2019; Zhang et al., 2022; Zhang et al., 2019). However, by including more trials than these previous meta-analyses, we were able to detect statistically significant effect modification by treatment regimen for the first time with statistical significance (pinteraction=0.042). The pattern of intake could be important for a favourable steady state of the bioavailability of the active 1,25 (OH)₂D hormone. Daily administration counteracts the fast excretion of vitamin D from the circulation (Hollis and Wagner, 2013; Keum et al., 2022). Moreover, the enzymes CYP27B1 (converts 25(OH)D to 1,25 (OH)₂D) and CYP24A1 (inactivates 25(OH)D and 1,25(OH)₂D) follow first-order reaction kinetics (Vieth, 2009). This means that doubling the concentration of the precursor doubles the yield of the product, unlike other steroid hormones (e.g., cortisol, oestrogen, testosterone) that follow zero-order kinetics (Vieth, 2020). Intermittent, non-physiologically large vitamin D3 bolus doses may lead to unstable cycling of 25(OH)D and 1,25(OH)₂D levels in blood because the system needs time to adapt to the large doses (Hollis and Wagner, 2013; Keum et al., 2019; Vieth, 2020). In the long run, intermittent bolus regimens at weekly or larger intervals can lead to an up-regulation of countervailing factors (e.g., 24-hydroxylase (CYP24A1), 24,25(OH)2D and fibroblast growth factor 23), all of which ultimately leads to lower synthesis or higher degradation of 1,25(OH)₂D levels (Mazess et al., 2021). Bolus doses, unlike daily doses, failed to reduce C-reactive protein response and actually elevated anti-inflammatory cytokines and doubled the risk of hypercalcemia in previous studies (Krishnan et al., 2012; Martineau et al., 2017; Mazess et al., 2021).</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Oh no, up-regulation of fibroblast growth factor 23!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-23"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-23">[23]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I don't feel like I understand this deeply enough to have any opinion beyond the surface level that the body seems to adapt to large doses of vitamin D in ways that could possibly be bad.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-24"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-24">[24]</a></sup></span><span>It seems intuitive that small daily doses would be safer than gigantic monthly doses, but I'm always suspicious of post-hoc mechanistic speculation. Also, if people get enough sun, they can apparently synthesize 10,000-25,000 IU per day, which isn't that far from the 60,000 IU they got in the D-Health trial. But then again, I think Kunzia et al. are suggesting that the body is designed to adapt to </span><i><span>regular</span></i><span> exposure to large doses but not </span><i><span>intermittent</span></i><span> exposure?</span></p><p><span>Well, if you split up the trails by daily vs. bolus dosing, there's a decent pattern of daily dosing leading to better results:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Trial (daily dosing)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Cancer href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.83</span></a><span> (0.55 to 1.26)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article/97/2/614/2836424#:~:text=mortality%20%5Bhazard%20ratio%20%28HR%29%20%3D%200.93%3B%2095%25%20confidence%20interval%20%28CI%29%20%3D%200.85%E2%80%931.02%5D"><span>0.93</span></a><span> (0.85 to 1.02)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>VITAL (Manson 2018)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1809944#:~:text=0.83%3B%2095%25%20CI%2C%200.67%20to%201.02"><span>0.83</span></a><span> (0.67 to 1.02)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1809944#:~:text=0.99%3B%2095%25%20CI%2C%200.87%20to%201.12"><span>0.99</span></a><span> (0.87 to 1.12)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>D2d (Pittas 2019)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.23</span></a><span> (0.03 to 1.86)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>FIND (Virtanen 2022)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>1.14</span></a><span> (0.56 to 2.33)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9071497/#:~:text=0.81%20%280.32%E2%80%932.06%29"><span>0.81 </span></a><span>(0.32 to 2.06)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Trial (bolus dosing)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Trivedi 2003</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#SD1:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.86</span></a><span> (0.61 to 1.21)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3/references#CD007470-fig-0007"><span>0.90</span></a><span> (0.77 to 1.07)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Lyons 2007</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3/references#CD007470-fig-0007"><span>0.99</span></a><span> (0.93 to 1.05)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>ViDA (Scragg 2018)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.99 </span></a><span>(0.60 to 1.64)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://academic.oup.com/jbmr/article/38/10/1391/7610360?guestAccessKey=#:~:text=1%2E12"><span>1.12</span></a><span> (0.79 to 1.58)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>D-Health (Neale 2022)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35026158/#:~:text=HR%20for%20cancer%20mortality%20was%201%C2%B715"><span>1.15</span></a><span> (0.96 to 1.39)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35026158/#:~:text=HR%20of%20vitamin%20D3%20effect%20on%20all-cause%20mortality%20was%201.04"><span>1.04</span></a><span> (0.93 to 1.18)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>If those bolus dosing trials didn't exist, I'd think this looked pretty good. So, maybe? Or maybe this is a story made up to hallucinate a positive trend. I would lean towards the latter theory, but there are papers like Mazess et al.'s </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1002/jbm4.10567"><span>"Vitamin D: Bolus is Bogus"</span></a><span>, that suggested this pattern </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> D-Health's dismal results came out. There are even </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.7861/clinmed.2021-0035"><span>some trials</span></a><span> that suggest bolus doses don't even work for treating rickets. So… I'm still not convinced. But maybe.</span></p><p><span>Aside: There are also many Mendelian randomization studies that look at correlations between health and genes that are related to vitamin D. But I don't think these provide much information, because the assumptions are shaky and the genes don't explain much of the variance.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-25"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-25">[25]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Where are we?</span></h2><p><span>Still with me? Here's a summary of the above 5200 words:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The body uses vitamin D in all sorts of weird and complicated ways. It's biologically plausible that vitamin D </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> matter beyond bone stuff with severe deficiency, but there's no convincing mechanistic evidence that it </span><i><span>is</span></i><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Vitamin D levels are strongly correlated with good health outcomes, but RCTs have conclusively shown that most of these correlations are non-causal.</span></li><li value="3"><span>RCTs haven't conclusively shown any benefit for anything beyond beyond bone stuff. At best, they've given weak evidence for hazard ratios slightly below one.</span></li></ul><p><span>So you might be wondering: Isn't that quite weak? Wasn't this post supposed to be a </span><i><span>defense</span></i><span> of vitamin D?</span></p><h2><span>The case for supplementing anyway</span></h2><h3><span>It's biologically plausible that vitamin D is good</span></h3><p><span>Everyone agrees that severe vitamin D deficiency (below ~25 nmol/L) is bad. It leads to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rickets"><span>rickets</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osteomalacia"><span>adult rickets</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osteoporosis"><span>osteoporosis</span></a><span>, muscle weakness or even—with profound deficiency—to seizures or cardiac arrhythmia. This makes sense, because below ~25 nmol/L, the kidneys have trouble converting storage vitamin D into active vitamin D, meaning you don't absorb enough calcium from food.</span></p><p><span>The question is if taking supplement to </span><i><span>further</span></i><span> raise your levels (say to 50 or 90 nmol/L) is important. We have no mechanistic proof, but it might be true, because many parts of the body use vitamin D as a local signal and because cells are at least somewhat sensitive to circulating storage levels. There's also this weird thing where parathyroid hormone continues to decline as vitamin D levels rise above ~25 nmol/L even while this seems to make little difference to how much active vitamin D the kidneys make.</span></p><p><span>Nothing in this world comes without trade-offs. Surely, supplementing vitamin D comes with </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> downsides. But it seems very unlikely that raising vitamin D levels to a "normal" level would cause more harm than benefit. Especially because…</span></p><h3><span>Humans evolved to have a lot of vitamin D</span></h3><p><span>According to Luxwolda et al.'s 2012 paper, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1017/s0007114511007161"><span>"Traditionally living populations in East Africa have a mean serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D concentration of 115 nmol/L"</span></a><span>, traditionally living populations in East Africa have a mean serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D concentration of 115 nmol/L.</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile, </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1007/s11657-012-0093-0"><span>Wahl et al. 2012</span></a><span> try to estimate mean levels around the world today:</span></p><img alt="wahl.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782243418/lexical_client_uploads/ilgggsrlvpisbqeexfwl.png" /><p><span>This map looks weird because of varying lifestyle, diet, supplementation, and needing to combine fragmented studies. But you get the idea. And remember, those are just averages. So there are </span><i><span>lots</span></i><span> of people with levels </span><i><span>far</span></i><span> lower than that in our evolutionary history.</span></p><p><span>Of course, just the fact that vitamin D levels have dropped doesn't mean it's important. Parasitic worm load, wood smoke inhalation, and cousin marriage have also dropped, but we aren't rushing to restore those to ancestral levels.</span></p><p><span>But there's another piece of evidence: After humans migrated out of East Africa, some of them evolved pale skin. Pale skin is bad, because it allows light to destroy folate, which is crucial for pregnancy.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-26"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-26">[26]</a></sup></span><span>Evolution doesn't typically do things that harm fertility, because evolution wants to increase reproductive fitness. The </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1152/ajpregu.00136.2019"><span>most common explanation</span></a><span> is that pale skin allows more UV light to penetrate, and thus allows people to synthesize more vitamin D. If evolution was willing to pay the high "price" of folate destruction for more vitamin D, that seems like good evidence that vitamin D is important.</span></p><p><span>Some even see contrasts like the Inuits versus Scandinavians as a kind of natural experiment: They lived at similar latitudes, but Inuits ate a diet with vitamin D (fatty fish and whale blubber) and Scandinavians didn't. The result is that Inuits have darker skin than Scandinavians.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-27"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-27">[27]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This is all speculative, and even if true, might be driven by severe deficiency and rickets. Or perhaps prehistoric benefits don't translate to your lifestyle. But </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> the people in Luxwolda's sample in East Africa had levels above ~60 nmol/L. I just don't see how you can look at this and not see it as providing </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> suggestive evidence in favor of the idea that raising levels above severe deficiency is unlikely to be harmful, and could be important. So I think the prior is favorable.</span></p><h3><span>What do you expect from vitamin D?</span></h3><p><span>A hazard ratio like HR = 0.96 doesn't look very impressive. But hold on. Suppose that life expectancy is 80 years and that taking vitamin D every day reduces your risk of all-cause mortality by a factor of </span><i><span>HR</span></i><span>. A reasonable approximation in rich countries is that this would increase your life expectancy by</span></p><p><span>    </span><i><span>80 × 0.15 × (1-HR)</span></i><span> years </span><i><span>= 12 × (1-HR)</span></i><span> years,</span></p><p><span>where 0.15 is derived from the entropy of lifespan in rich countries.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-28"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-28">[28]</a></sup></span><span>For example, if all-cause mortality had a true hazard ratio of HR = 0.96, then taking vitamin D every day of your life would increase life expectancy by around</span></p><p><span>    </span><i><span>0.48</span></i><span> years.</span></p><p><span>I claim that this would be a lot. Certainly, if I were about to face my destiny, I would pay a lot of money for an extra 0.48 years. Or, you can calculate that this corresponds to an increase of life expectancy </span><i><span>per-vitamin-D-pill</span></i><span> of 8.6 minutes.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-29"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-29">[29]</a></sup></span><span>A common rule-of-thumb is that smoking a cigarette costs around 11 minutes of life in expectation. If you think HR = 0.96 is trivial, do you also think that smoking one cigarette each day is fine?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-30"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-30">[30]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The correlational studies suggested that vitamin D might drop your risk of all-cause mortality by a third. It's disappointing that the RCTs refuted this. But those correlational studies were crazy. They imply</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-31"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-31">[31]</a></sup></span><span>an increase of life expectancy of around 4 years or around 6.5 cigarettes per day. Could we really believe that you could smoke 6.5 cigarettes, then take a vitamin D pill, and you're even?</span></p><p><span>Personally, I think hazard ratios just slightly less than one are the best we can reasonably hope for. But I also think that they would be an </span><i><span>excellent</span></i><span> return on investment. Arguably, modern human life expectancy comes from stacking lots of modest hazard ratios on top of each other.</span></p><h3><span>What do you expect from vitamin D trials?</span></h3><p><span>Let's play a game. Let's hallucinate some numbers for what vitamin D might do, and then simulate what trials would show. Here are the strongest effects I consider plausible for different baseline levels, along with </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ajcnut.2025.09.046"><span>how common</span></a><span> those levels are in the United States.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Storage vitamin D (nmol/L)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Hazard ratio</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>% of population</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>&lt;30</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.75</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>30-49</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.92</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>15</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>50-125</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.98</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>72.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>&gt;125</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>7.5</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Suppose that were real. Now, say we pick 26,000 people at random, and give half of them vitamin D for give yars. Here are the results of a million simulated trials, assuming a baseline mortality risk of 0.7%: </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-32"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-32">[32]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="rr.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782243449/lexical_client_uploads/ivga507m1uzqi86qtlcl.png" /><p><span>Overall, 9% of trials would find a significant benefit, 63% would find a non-significant benefit, 27% would find a non-significant harm, and 1% would find a significant harm.</span></p><p><span>If you wanted to have an 80% chance of finding a significant decrease, you'd need to run a trial with something like 570,000 people, almost five times more than in all the above trials combined.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-33"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-33">[33]</a></sup></span><span>If you don't like my numbers, I've put up a </span><a href="https://dynomight.net/img/vitamin-d/sim.html"><span>page</span></a><span> where you can run your own simulations with different ones.</span></p><p><span>My point is, the results we see in vitamin D RCTs are what we should </span><i><span>expect</span></i><span> to see if vitamin D had plausible benefits. That's not proof, of course—just that if you start with realistic expectations, the trials don't provide much evidence in either direction.</span></p><h3><span>The trials do find </span><i><span>slightly</span></i><span> helpful numbers</span></h3><p><span>Recent meta-analyses have not consistently found a statistically significant benefit to vitamin D supplementation. But they do </span><i><span>suggest</span></i><span> a small benefit for cancer mortality and all-cause mortality, and they're </span><i><span>close</span></i><span> to being statistically significant. That's </span><i><span>something</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>And if you buy the argument that bolus dosing is bad, the results get even better. Kunzia et al. did a meta-analysis of cancer mortality using only trials with daily dosing, and found a hazard ratio of 0.88 (confidence interval 0.78 to 0.98). I'd keep this at arm's length. The bolus dosing trials might have done worse by random chance, meaning this is a kind of p-hacking. But there's a reasonable chance (maybe 25-50%) that bolus dosing really is bad, in which case those trials would be convincing evidence.</span></p><p><span>I actually think it's surprising that the meta-analyses look as good as they do, because there just aren't that many people who started out with low vitamin D levels. Only a handful of trials had mean levels below 60 nmol/L, and they all give semi-promising results:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-34"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-34">[34]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Trial (low-ish baseline)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Trivedi 2003</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#SD1:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.86</span></a><span> (0.61 to 1.21)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3/references#CD007470-fig-0007"><span>0.90</span></a><span> (0.77 to 1.07)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>WHI (Jackson 2006)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#SD1:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.89</span></a><span> (0.77 to 1.03)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3882746/#:~:text=FIG%2E%201"><span>0.92</span></a><span> (0.83 to 1.01)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Lyons 2007</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3/references#CD007470-fig-0007"><span>0.99</span></a><span> (0.93 to 1.05)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>RECORD (Avenell 2012)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10214278/#:~:text=Fig%2E%202,-%2E"><span>0.83</span></a><span> (0.55 to 1.26)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article/97/2/614/2836424#:~:text=mortality%20%5Bhazard%20ratio%20%28HR%29%20%3D%200.93%3B%2095%25%20confidence%20interval%20%28CI%29%20%3D%200.85%E2%80%931.02%5D"><span>0.93</span></a><span> (0.85 to 1.02)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Again, it's dangerous to dig too deeply looking for these kinds of patterns. If you dig enough, you can always find a way to confirm whatever theory you want. But also again, maybe?</span></p><h3><span>You're probably already taking vitamin D</span></h3><p><span>You might not personally supplement vitamin D. But for most people reading this, someone else is supplementing it for you.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-35"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-35">[35]</a></sup></span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Country</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Commonly fortified with vitamin D</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Australia</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Belgium</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Canada</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Milk, margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Chile</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Milk, flour</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Ethiopia</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Oils</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Finland</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Milk, yogurt, margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Ireland</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine, cereal</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>New Zealand</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine (from Australia)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Norway</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine, low-fat milk</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Pakistan</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Oils</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Poland</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Sweden</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Milk, yogurt, plant milk, margarine</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>United Kingdom</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Margarine, cereal</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>United States</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Milk, plant milk, margarine, cereal, yogurt</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>Fortified food is common across the Anglosphere and Scandinavian peninsula. However, it's rare in the rest of Europe (exceptions: Belgium, Poland) and even-more rare in the rest of the world (exceptions: Chile, Ethiopia, Pakistan).</span></p><p><span>I think this is important for two reasons. First, vitamin D is oddly self-defeating. There are some places in the world where people care about vitamin D. These are the places that run large trials. But these places also fortify their food and tend to be full of people that already supplement vitamin D. These places also tend to believe it's unethical to tell the control group not to take vitamin D.</span></p><p><span>And here's another question: If you think vitamin D is worthless, are you comfortable recommending </span><i><span>removing</span></i><span> vitamin D from food? If not, then why is the particular amount of fortification in food now the right one?</span></p><p><span>Some might argue that the purpose of fortification is to reach the severely deficient, or children, the elderly or pregnant mothers. Maybe! But again, if you could press a button and remove fortification from everyone else, would you feel comfortable pushing that button? Remember, trials don't test going down from current levels, only going up.</span></p><h2><span>So that's my story</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>Biology and evolution suggest a prior that moderate levels of vitamin D (say 80 nmol/L) are quite possibly better than low levels (like 40 nmol/L) and unlikely to be worse.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Observational studies say that vitamin D is magical, but those studies are bad and we should ignore them.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The RCTs show that vitamin D is non-miraculous. But beyond that they don't provide much information, because they mostly enrolled people with moderate vitamin D levels, meaning plausible effects would require colossal sample sizes to reliably detect.</span></li><li value="4"><span>What evidence the RCTs do provide points weakly towards a modest benefit.</span></li><li value="5"><span>If real, that benefit would far exceed the cost of taking vitamin D.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Therefore, if you have low vitamin D, it seems wise to supplement.</span></li></ul><p><span>This is all very weak, I know! But sometimes weak evidence is all we've got.</span></p><p><span>I wish we had at least </span><i><span>one</span></i><span> large trial done in a population with low starting levels. But as far as I can tell, none are underway. In fact, it's </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsbmb.2025.106895"><span>unlikely</span></a><span> that there will be any more large trials anytime soon. So weak evidence is how it's going to be.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-1"><p><span>Technically, vitamin D itself is a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secosteroid"><span>type of steroid</span></a><span> although not what people usually mean by "steroid". </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-2"><p><span>Here are some of the fancy names for the different forms of vitamin D I'll talk about:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>my name</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>fancy names</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>provitamin D</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7-Dehydrocholesterol"><span>7-dehydrocholesterol</span></a></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>previtamin D</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Previtamin_D3"><span>previtamin D₃</span></a></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>vitamin D</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitamin_D"><span>cholecalciferol</span></a></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>storage vitamin D</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcifediol"><span>calcifediol</span></a><span> / </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ergocalciferol"><span>ergocalciferol</span></a><span> / 25(OH)D / 25-hydroxyvitamin D</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>active vitamin D</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcitriol"><span>calcitriol</span></a><span> / ercalcitriol / 1,25(OH)₂D / 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-3"><p><span>Charmingly named </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitamin_D-binding_protein"><span>"vitamin D-binding protein"</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-3"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-4"><p><span>If you eat mushrooms or yeast, it joins the vitamin D from your skin en route to your liver. If you eat animals or animal products, you also get some storage vitamin D, which doesn't need to be processed by the liver. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-4"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-5"><p><span>Storage vitamin D is what your doctor measures in your blood test. This is sometimes measured in nmol/L and sometimes in ng/mL. The latter measurement is smaller by a factor of 2.496. So 25 nmol/L ≈ 10 ng/mL. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-5"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-6"><p><span>Apperly was building on a </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1097/00000441-193709000-00004"><span>1937 paper</span></a><span> that observed observed that sailors, exposed to lots of sunlight, had much higher skin cancer rates than the general population, but lower overall cancer rates. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-6"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-7"><p><span>I theorize that the Garland brothers are alive and writing </span><a href="https://slimemoldtimemold.com/"><span>Slime Mold Time Mold</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-7"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-8"><p><span>In Biologist, active vitamin D is not just an "endocrine" hormone that sends signals for far away cells through the blood, it's also a "paracrine" or "autocrine" hormone that sends signals to nearby cells or inside a single cell, through diffusion. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-8"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-9"><p><span>You might ask, why is vitamin D used by so many different parts of the body for so many different purposes?</span></p><p><span>I think there's no deep answer here. It's true for the same reason that dogs sneeze to signal that they're feeling playful: Evolution re-uses stuff for different purposes all the time. Imagine that DNA already exists coding for the vitamin D receptor and for the enzyme to convert storage vitamin D into active vitamin D. If some cells need to send a local signal, re-using those is easier than inventing something new. There's nothing unusual or magical about this. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-9"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-10"><p><span>Don't try to make sense of this. It doesn't make sense.</span></p><p><span>You </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> speculate that this is because the parathyroid glands are trying to make less active vitamin D to compensate for the fact that vitamin-D receptors throughout the body are sensitive to storage vitamin D itself. But I advise against. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-10"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-11"><p><span>400 IU is the recommended daily amount </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-11"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-12"><p><span>The WHI trial was a pioneer in salami-slicing results for different outcomes into dozens of different papers, most of which are hard to access. All trials now seem to have adopted this hideous trend which makes it </span><i><span>maddening</span></i><span> to try to summarize what actually happened in a trial. Also, slightly different numbers for the same quantity appear in different places. I haven't bothered to chase these down, because the differences are all very small, e.g. a hazard ratio of 0.89 for cancer mortality rather than 0.90. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-12"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-13"><p><span>Guess what most kidney stones are made of? </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-13"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-14"><p><span>Half of the vitamin D group and the placebo group also got omega 3. These are averaged together in the results. Also, VITAL carefully stratified the assignment to vitamin D or placebo based on baseline vitamin D levels, which should give more statistical power from a given sample size. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-14"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-15"><p><span>There was also a </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12308094/"><span>weird study</span></a><span> done on a subset of 1031 people from the VITAL population that looked at telomere length. After starting with around 8700 base pairs, the control group lost around 160 base pairs during the study, while the vitamin D group only lost an average of 20. I'm not sure of what to make of this. For one thing, though the authors claim this is statistically significant, it depends on how you analyze the data. But beyond that, sure, telomere length is a marker of aging, but telomeres get shorter for a reason (likely to fight cancer) and it isn't obvious that slowing this would always be a good thing. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-15"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-16"><p><span>This is a little complicated. In VITAL, participants were only eligible if they were taking at most 800 IU per day, and they were restricted to 800 IU per day during the trial. In D-health, participants were only eligible if they were taking at most 500 IU per day, but they were allowed to take up to 2000 IU per day during the trial. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-16"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-17"><p><span>You might ask: If vitamin D only has a modest effect, then why is it so strongly correlated with health?</span></p><p><span>In principle, I'd like to </span><a href="https://dynomight.net/control/"><span>push back</span></a><span> against the idea that we need to explain why these </span><i><span>particular</span></i><span> correlations don't imply causation. But the accepted explanation is a combination of (1) reverse causation where being healthy causes people to spend more time outside and thus get more vitamin D; (2) confounding, where obesity is bad for you and leads to lower measured vitamin D levels; (3) confounding, where more healthy lifestyles lead to both more vitamin D and more health; and (4) confounding, where higher socioeconomic status leads to both more vitamin D and more health. You might ask why these correlations would be true at a state level like the Garlands looked at, but then you run into the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_fallacy"><span>ecological fallacy</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modifiable_areal_unit_problem"><span>modifiable areal unit problem</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-17"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-18"><p><span>I took all the trials that got at least 2% weight and were rated as "low risk of bias" in this </span><a href="https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007470.pub3/references#CD007470-fig-0007"><span>2014 Cochrane review</span></a><span> of vitamin D and mortality, then manually added all the "major" trials that were published after 2014.</span></p><p><span>I shudder to think of the time it took to make this table. I tried using AI but found it was wildly unreliable. Part of the problem is that each trial's results are distributed among many papers, in different journals, with different paywalls. And many details aren't published at all by the original authors but are only scrounged up and put in the depths of the supplementary material of a review years later. In some cases, different sources also give contradictory numbers. The differences were always tiny (e.g. 0.90 rather than 0.89) but it still makes me nervous. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-18"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" class="table-cell"><p><b><span>VITAL 2018</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2,000 IU daily</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>800</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>DO-HEALTH 2020</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2,000 IU daily</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1000 / 800 (screening / trial)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>55 ± 22</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>100 ± 27</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>D-Health 2022</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>60,000 IU monthly</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>500 / 2000 (screening / trial)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>77 ± 25 (predicted)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>115 ± 30</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>FIND 2022</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1,600 or 3,200 IU daily</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>800</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>75 ± 18</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>100 ± 21 or 120 ± 22</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-19"><span>↩︎</span></a></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-20"><p><span>Among the major trials, only VITAL, ViDA, and FIND measured it for more than a tiny number of subjects. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-20"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-21"><p><span>In VITAL and ViDA, people with baseline levels below 50 nmol/L had a </span><i><span>higher</span></i><span> hazard ratio for cancer mortality (though with wide confidence intervals), suggesting if anything less benefit. Or, you could use race as a proxy for baseline vitamin D. But in both VITAL and WHI, the hazard ratio for cancer mortality was higher among non-Whites. After looking at many such analyses for many outcomes, the only clear result I could find was for diabetes in the D2d trail, where the hazard ratio was </span><i><span>much</span></i><span> lower for people below 30 nmol/L (0.38 vs. 0.93). </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-21"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-22"><p><span>The results for VITAL look decent:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>outcome (VITAL trial)</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>HR</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>HR excluding first two years</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.96 (0.88 to 1.06)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.94 (0.83 to 1.06)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Cancer mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.83 (0.67 to 1.02)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.75 (0.59 to 0.96)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Major CVD event</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.97 (0.85 to 1.12)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.93 (0.79 to 1.09)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>All-cause mortality</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.99 (0.87 to 1.12)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>0.96 (0.84 to 1.11)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>But in D-Health, excluding the first two years actually increased the hazard ratio for cancer mortality from </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35026158/#:~:text=HR%20for%20cancer%20mortality%20was%201%C2%B715"><span>1.15</span></a><span> (0.96 to 1.39) to </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35026158/#:~:text=1%C2%B724%20%5B95%25%20CI%201%C2%B701%2D1%C2%B754%5D%3B"><span>1.24</span></a><span> (1.01 to 1.54). Most other trials were too short for this kind of analysis to make sense. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-22"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-23"><p><span>That could downregulate 25-hydroxyvitamin D 1-alpha-hydroxylase, reducing the rate it catalyzes the hydroxylation of hydroxycholecalciferol into 1,25-dihydroxycholecalciferol! </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-23"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-24"><p><span>Dynomight: WTF is this?</span></p><p><span>Dynomight Biologist: Well, C-reactive protein is generally considered inflammatory.</span></p><p><span>Dynomight: So reducing that is good? But then why do they talk like elevating </span><i><span>anti</span></i><span>-inflammatory cytokines would be bad?</span></p><p><span>Dynomight Biologist: Yeah… That would be good. Unless you have cancer. In which case it's not good.</span></p><p><span>Dynomight: OK! </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-24"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-25"><p><span>Mendelian randomization studies are based on the idea that certain genes predispose you to have higher levels of circulating vitamin D. If you assume that those genes are randomly distributed in the population and have no effects other than affecting vitamin D, then they serve as a kind of natural experiment. With vitamin D, these studies typically show </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/S2213-8587(23)00287-5"><span>null results</span></a><span>. However, the validity of the assumptions is debatable and the identified genes only explain ~5% of the variance in vitamin D levels, which makes the results very noisy. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-25"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-26"><p><span>Pale skin also greatly increases the risk of sunburn and skin cancer. In the US, White people get melanoma at around </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ypmed.2016.08.032"><span>25 times</span></a><span> the rate of Black people, despite (I assume) higher usage of sunscreen and better health outcomes in most other dimensions. But experts generally think folate deficiency created stronger selective pressure, since it's so closely linked to reproduction. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-26"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-27"><p><span>It's a more complicated than this, because you also need to look at the amount of folate in diet, as well as migration patterns and how long populations had to adapt to their environment. But experts seem to consider this the leading explanation for the evolution of pale skin. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-27"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-28"><p><span>To derive this, suppose that </span><i><span>S(t)</span></i><span> is the probability that someone survives to age t. Then life expectancy is </span><i><span>∫ S(t) dt</span></i><span>, where the integral runs from 0 to ∞. If you change the hazard ratio by a factor of </span><i><span>HR</span></i><span>, then the new in life expectancy is </span><i><span>L(HR) = ∫ S(t)ᴴᴿ dt</span></i><span>, so the change under a linear approximation is </span><i><span>ΔL ≈ (HR-1) × L'(1)</span></i><span>. This is more commonly written as </span><i><span>ΔL ≈ (HR-1) × L(1) × H</span></i><span>, where </span><i><span>H = -L'(1)/L(1)</span></i><span> is known as the </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6368683/"><span>Keyfitz entropy</span></a><span>. This is is chosen because the quantity </span><i><span>H</span></i><span> is relatively stable, and in rich countries is typically between 0.10 and 0.20. So a decent estimate would be that baseline life expectancy is </span><i><span>L(1)=80</span></i><span> years and </span><i><span>H = 0.15</span></i><span> in which case the change in life expectancy is around </span><i><span>12 × (1-HR)</span></i><span> years. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-28"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-29"><p><span>Observe that 0.48 years is 252460.8 minutes. Assuming you lived for 80 years and took a pill every day of your life, that would be 80 * 365.25 = 29220 pills. 252460.8 minutes / 29220 pills = 8.64 minutes/pill. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-29"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-30"><p><span>I expect that a number of you are happy to bite that bullet and say yes, HR=0.96 is trivial and smoking a cigarette each day is also fine. I don't personally agree, but it's not my place to question your utility function and I applaud your consistency. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-30"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-31"><p><span>A hazard ratio of HR=2/3, implies a change in life expectancy of 12 × (1 - 1/3) years = 4 years or 2,103,840 minutes. That corresponds to a per-pill increase of 2,103,840 minutes / 29,220 pills = 72 minutes/pill. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-31"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-32"><p><span>Technically, this is calculating a relative risk rather than a hazard ratio, but I think the difference isn't very significant given that we're assuming a uniform mortality risk. I used AI to create that simulation, though I did test that it replicates a traditional </span><a href="https://www.stat.ubc.ca/~rollin/stats/ssize/b2.html"><span>power calculator</span></a><span> across a wide range of parameters when the relative risk is constant for all vitamin D levels. So I mostly trust it. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-32"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-33"><p><span>This simulation is probably a bit pessimistic. Things look a bit better if you use an older population where baseline mortality is higher. (Almost all trials do.) In principle, you could also use a population where more people have low levels, which could help a lot. But, for whatever reason, almost no trials do that. In fact, most trials accidentally </span><i><span>under</span></i><span>-sample people with low vitamin D, because people who agree to participate tend to be more health-conscious. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-33"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-34"><p><span>Kunzia et al. made a heroic effort to contact study authors and get data for individual patients. After getting data for 21,558 people (almost all from ViDA + FIND + VITAL + WHI) only 3,663 had levels below 50 nmol/L. That's not enough to reliably detect a modest effect, meaning their confidence interval for this group is gigantic. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-34"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-35"><p><span>In this table, I tried to capture foods that are commonly fortified in practice, not just when it's legally required. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-9g5obYHAyF7ApMSfZ-35"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sF5gAxnmifQe2TBNt/the-worthlessness-of-vitamin-d-is-mildly-exaggerated#comments">Discuss</a>

---
